# DONE

Closed backlog items, newest first. One line per closed part, in the same shape as `TODO.md`: the permanent
id, what it was, and how it ended. Ids are never reused for a different item. When only part of an item is retired,
its doable remainder stays in `TODO.md` under the same id; the entry here closes only the retired part. An
id may therefore appear once per pull request that retires part of it: the pair of id and pull request
identifies a row (maintainer ruling, 2026-10-21). One older row instead names a range of pull requests, and rows
that a maintainer ruling retired without a pull request name the ruling's date. A few older rows name no pull
request and are the only row for their id.

**Done** means the work shipped. **Dropped** means it was considered and declined. **Retired** means
a maintainer ruling ended the requirement without claiming the work ran. Each row says why it ended,
because an item that simply vanishes gets proposed again six months later.

| ID | Item | Ended |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1.302 | `ollama.md`: profiling, dependency-module pprof reachability by source reading of the 93 `go.sum` modules in the 22 October 2026 module cache, partial. (M, M) | Done, #932. Read, with no Go code built or run, the extracted module directories of the 93 module zips that v0.34.2's `go.sum` lists, from the `cache/download` tree of the 22 October 2026 sweep's module cache, reused under the maintainer ruling of 2026-10-26. Each of those zips has a `.ziphash` and an `h1:` hash recomputed from its contents that match `go.sum`. The other 15 zips in that cache, including `golang.org/x/tools` v0.38.0, have no `go.sum` line, so the reading excluded them. Ignoring build constraints and `_test.go` files, only `package main` files in the 93 modules import `net/http/pprof` or `runtime/pprof`: seven `github.com/dgryski/trifles` commands, `github.com/klauspost/compress/s2/cmd/s2c` and `golang.org/x/text/collate/tools/colcmp`. The text `/debug/pprof` appears only in `github.com/dgryski/trifles/fastpprof`, a `package main` command, and in a `github.com/pelletier/go-toml/v2` test file. Imports followed from every main-module package through the files of packages other than `package main`, with standard-library packages as leaves, reach no pprof importer and no import path outside the 93 modules and the standard library. The `golang.org/x/text` and `golang.org/x/exp` packages that import `golang.org/x/tools` packages are imported only by `package main` and test files. Under the maintainer's claims ruling of the same date, rewrote the `profiling-deps-scan`, `profiling-scope` and `profiling-tag-runs` limits, still REASONED, as this guide's inference that no `go.sum` dependency module adds either import to any v0.34.2 build at those module versions, and updated the C9 provenance, served-mux and limits text. Added the `go.sum` Sources item with the `ollama` key `sfc02278c501f`. No claim was added or removed, so the header's claim count is unchanged; rebinding `body_sha256` and regenerating the summary and bundles go with this change. Other Go versions, the standard library's importers beyond one linux/amd64 query, whether the failed builds load, served-mux exposure in such builds and shipped-binary pprof availability remain open in TODO.md under 1.302. |

| 1.301 | `observability-components.md`: the write-quota and transport-close cases of the ingester's empty-batch send, the named stream interceptors and the compactor's delete-request processing, from the 2026-10-26 qa-p238a pack, partial. (M, M) | Done, #927. The qa-p238a pack holds 20 Loki v3.7.8 files, fetched on 2026-10-26 between 12:26:54Z and 12:26:59Z, and the v3.7.8 tag archive, fetched at 12:27:15Z (`fetch.log` lines 1 to 21), which this change does not use. Nothing was built or run, and the files were not compared with the pack's `SHA256SUMS`. As source reading with the p206a pack, a write-quota wait ends only when quota returns or the done channel of the context the transport created for the stream closes (`flowcontrol.go` lines 47 to 67; `http2_server.go` lines 623 and 624). An enqueue on the control buffer fails, with `ErrConnClosing`, only once the buffer is finished (`controlbuf.go` lines 348 to 366 and 454 to 461). `Close` finishes the buffer before it closes the transport's `done` channel and cancels the streams (`http2_server.go` lines 1299 to 1312), and the loopy writer, which the server transport runs in its own goroutine (`http2_server.go` lines 348 to 350), finishes the buffer when it exits (`controlbuf.go` lines 584 to 593). So if the context ends after the transport's state checks, a send whose write-quota wait succeeds then puts its data frame on the control buffer and returns no error only if that enqueue succeeds; once the buffer is finished, `put` returns `ErrConnClosing`, which `write` returns (`http2_server.go` lines 1178 to 1187; `controlbuf.go` lines 348 to 366). After a failed wait, `write` returns `ErrConnClosing` if the transport's `done` channel is closed and otherwise `ContextErr` of the stream's current context (lines 1010 to 1017 and 1178 to 1180). If the transport closes before the stream is marked done, a send whose frames are enqueued before the control buffer is finished returns no error, and a later one fails with `ErrConnClosing`. The header write turns that error into an `Unavailable` status (lines 1038 to 1044 and 1065 to 1069), and so does `toRPCErr` (`rpc_util.go` lines 1145 to 1147). In dskit's `server.go`, otelgrpc's server stats handler is always installed, the in-flight limiter is added when `GrpcMethodLimiter` is set, and dskit's own stats handler when stats tracking is enabled (lines 483 to 485 and 491 to 523). The limiter's `TagRPC` returns its input (`limits.go` lines 151 to 153), dskit's stats handler's returns a `context.WithValue` child (`grpc_stats.go` lines 56 to 58), and otelgrpc's returns a `context.WithValue` child of the context its propagator extraction and tracer start return (`stats_handler.go` lines 98 to 158). That extraction and tracer start, grpc-go's `istats.NewCombinedHandler` (grpc-go's `server.go` line 715) and any stats handler that `GRPCOptions` or global server options add were not read, so the context's state after a failed wait on an open transport, and the status code that follows, stay open. The query-tags, HTTP-header, ingestion-policy and query-limits stream interceptors each pass the handler a wrapper that replaces only `Context` and return the handler's error unchanged (`grpc_query_tags.go` lines 66 to 81; `grpc_headers.go` lines 56 to 61; `ingestion_policies.go` lines 256 to 312; `querylimits/grpc.go` lines 63 to 122). The query-limits one also returns a metadata parse error before the handler runs (`querylimits/grpc.go` lines 103 to 107). With authentication off, `fakeauth.SetupAuthMiddleware` appends a fake-tenant stream interceptor of the same shape (`fake_auth.go` lines 15, 16, 42 to 48 and 63 to 78). With authentication on, that function appends one that passes listed methods' streams through unchanged and sends the rest to dskit's user-header interceptor (lines 17 to 39). The OpenTracing stream interceptor that dskit adds passes the stream itself or a wrapper that replaces only `Context`, and returns the handler's error unchanged (the vendored `opentracing-contrib/go-grpc/server.go` lines 91 to 135). The recovery stream interceptor is built by the vendored go-grpc-middleware recovery package (`recovery.go` line 39), whose code neither pack holds, so whether it wraps `SendMsg` stays open. For deletion, the documentation page says deletion follows a configurable cancellation period, default 24h, and that `filter-only` requests are filtered at query time but not removed from storage (`logs-deletion.md` lines 16, 34 and 37). The delete-requests manager selects unprocessed requests only after the cancellation period plus one minute and only for tenants in `filter-and-delete` mode, tries to mark one whose interval lies wholly beyond the tenant's longest retention processed without filtering, and loads at most the batch size (`delete_requests_manager.go` lines 332 to 457 and 667 to 683). Each attempt to mark a request processed calls the delete-request store's `MarkShardAsProcessed`, and on a store error the manager logs the error and returns nothing to the caller, which carries on (`delete_requests_manager.go` lines 530 to 550). When a mark phase starts, the manager loads that batch, in the main horizontal-scaling mode only requests without a line filter; when the phase finishes it tries to mark each loaded request processed, and a failed or timed-out phase only resets the batch (lines 504 to 584). The compactor passes the manager to the retention marker behind its retention expiration checker (`compactor.go` lines 309, 391 and 561 to 567). In the main horizontal-scaling mode, the manager builds a deletion manifest of line-filter requests every five minutes (`delete_requests_manager.go` lines 201 to 288). The job builder turns each manifest segment into jobs of up to 1000 chunks and cancels the manifest on a failed job (`job_builder.go` lines 26, 116 to 239 and 323 to 403). After passing the collected storage updates to the tables manager, the job builder tries to mark the manifest's requests processed through the manager's callback, which returns nothing, and then goes on to delete the manifest's files whatever the marks' outcome (`job_builder.go` lines 142 to 150 and 405 to 437; `delete_requests_manager.go` lines 124 to 129). Given a job, the deletion job runner examines each chunk the job lists. The runner skips a listed chunk that storage reports as not found (`job_runner.go` lines 90 to 99) and leaves a found chunk unchanged when no request's filter matches any of its lines (lines 117 to 143). Otherwise the runner rewrites the found chunk without the matched lines, recording a chunk left empty with no replacement and uploading a rebuilt one that stays within the table's range (lines 54 to 203). The files read hold no caller of `deletion.NewJobRunner` and no `RegisterJobRunner` call for deletion jobs, and the job queue's dispatch was not read, so whether deletion jobs reach that runner stays open. The Loki section now says so, with REASONED labels and no run claimed. Twenty-nine Sources items without metadata keys, since no claim cites them, cite these lines; no claim changed, so the header and summary are unchanged. Rebinding `body_sha256` and regenerating the bundles go with this change. The context's state after a failed write-quota wait on an open transport, the recovery interceptor, the code that runs the mark phases, the retention marker and sweeper, the tables manager and the manifest builder stay open in TODO.md under 1.301 with the row's other items. So do the job queue's dispatch of deletion jobs, the worker's registration of the deletion job runner, the delete-request filter and the store backends. |
| 1.283 | `agent-builders.md`: debug-remedy, the documentation search for which built-in enterprise workspace roles Dify publishes as holding `plugin.plugin_preferences`, from the 2026-10-26 p238a pack, partial. (M, M) | Done, #928. The p238a pack holds Dify's English Self-Host and Cloud documentation indexes, `https://docs.dify.ai/_llms/en/self-host.txt` and `https://docs.dify.ai/_llms/en/cloud.txt`, both fetched on 2026-10-26 at 12:27:16Z with status 200 (`fetch.log` lines 1 and 2). The pack also holds the Self-Host and Cloud Manage Members pages, Integrations pages (`workspace/plugins.md`) and Developer Resources indexes, fetched on 2026-10-26 from 12:54:20Z to 12:54:22Z with status 200 (`fetch.log` lines 3 to 8); nothing was run. All eight files' SHA-256 digests match the pack's `SHA256SUMS`. None of the eight URLs is pinned, so later versions were not read. Both documentation indexes list page titles, most with a one-line description. A case-insensitive search of both for `rbac`, `enterprise`, `permission`, `role`, `member`, `preference` and `premium` matched six entries. The six matches are the Self-Host `Dify Premium on AWS` entry (`self-host.txt` line 30) and `Collaborate with Teammates` entry (line 58), the two `Manage Members` entries (`self-host.txt` line 203, `cloud.txt` line 171) and the two `Personal Settings` entries (`self-host.txt` line 204, `cloud.txt` line 172). Neither index names RBAC or an enterprise role, or maps any role to `plugin.plugin_preferences` or another permission key. Both `Manage Members` entries describe their page as one to "Add teammates to your workspace and manage the roles that control their access", at `https://docs.dify.ai/en/self-host/use-dify/workspace/team-members-management.md` and `https://docs.dify.ai/en/cloud/use-dify/workspace/team-members-management.md`. Both Manage Members pages list four built-in roles, Owner, Admin, Editor and Normal, and name no plugin permission for any; Admin is to "Manage members and model providers, plus everything an Editor can do", and the Integrations pages list model providers as an integration type (`team-members-management.md` Self-Host lines 13 to 18, Cloud lines 23 to 28; `plugins.md` line 15 of each). The Self-Host Manage Members page adds that Dify Enterprise custom roles give "granular access to apps and knowledge bases" (line 26). Both Integrations pages say "The workspace owner and admins control who can install and debug integrations from **Permissions**", each permission going to everyone, admins only or no one, and that only the owner and admins can remove integrations (`plugins.md` Self-Host lines 53 to 55, Cloud lines 47 to 49). The Self-Host Integrations page adds that on Dify Enterprise "admins can restrict installation across the deployment" (line 49), and both Integrations pages describe per-category auto-update strategies without naming who may set them (Self-Host line 59, Cloud line 53). The Integrations pages establish published owner and admin control of the install and debug permissions and of removal but do not name `plugin.plugin_preferences` or RBAC, say whether the owner and admin roles are the enterprise RBAC roles, or map any built-in enterprise workspace role to the key. A case-insensitive search of both Developer Resources indexes matched `permission` only on the plugin `Manifest` entry (Self-Host line 210, Cloud line 217), `preference` only as "preferred" on the `Multilingual README` entry (Self-Host line 211, Cloud line 218) and `role` nowhere; `plugin` matched plugin-development headings and entries and one knowledge-pipeline API entry (line 162 of each). The pages those two indexes list were not read, so the silence of the pages read does not establish that Dify publishes no such mapping elsewhere. No guide text, claim, Sources item or `body_sha256` changed. Since none of the pages read maps a built-in enterprise workspace role to the key, the ruling of 2026-10-25T12:57:57Z sends the enterprise-role item to the maintainer to retire or move to observation, and the item stays open in TODO.md under 1.283 awaiting that ruling. The model service's handling of enable and disable, the detail header's `canUpdatePlugin` input and whether any route reaches `ProviderList`'s built-in tab also stay open there. |
| 1.300 | `n8n.md`: environment, the qa-p236a source reading of environment preparation and the launch command at every launcher tag from 0.1.0 to 1.5.1, partial. (M, M) | Done, #925. The qa-p236a pack holds `git archive` trees of all 29 tags of the n8n-io/task-runner-launcher repository, which the integrator listed with their commits at 2026-10-26T10:23:55Z (`tags.txt`; newest 1.5.1), and the integrator's per-tag hashes of `internal/env/env.go` and `internal/commands/launch.go` (`file-hashes.txt`); nothing was built or run. Under the maintainer ruling of 2026-10-25T23:51:55Z, which bounds the item to these tags, each distinct version of `env.go` outside 1.3.1 to 1.5.1 and of `launch.go` outside 1.5.0 and 1.5.1 was read once, taking equal integrator hashes (the first 12 hex digits of SHA-256) as identical files. The tags 0.1.0 to 0.2.0-alpha have neither file and hold a Rust launcher in `src/main.rs`, read whole at 0.1.0, 0.1.1 and 0.2.0-alpha; 0.1.0-rc1 names the same commit as 0.1.0, and 0.1.1's file was compared with 0.1.0's by reading only, not by hash. 0.1.0, 0.1.0-rc1 and 0.1.1 switch to the configuration's uid and gid, keep only `LANG`, `PATH`, `TZ`, `TERM` and the `allowed-env` names from their own environment and pass that list alone to `execve`, adding no grant token (`tags/0.1.0/src/main.rs` lines 15 to 23 and 100 to 155). 0.2.0-alpha filters the same way and, only when `N8N_RUNNERS_AUTH_TOKEN` is set, fetches a grant token through `N8N_RUNNERS_N8N_URI`, drops any inherited `N8N_RUNNERS_GRANT_TOKEN` and adds the fetched one before `execve`; its uid and gid switch is commented out (`tags/0.2.0-alpha/src/main.rs` lines 102 to 132, 159, 191 to 207 and 238 to 256). 0.3.0-rc keeps the same four names and `allowed-env` (`tags/0.3.0-rc/internal/commands/launch.go` lines 67 to 69; `internal/env/env.go` lines 10 to 29). 0.4.0-rc and 0.5.0-rc also keep `N8N_RUNNERS_AUTO_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT` and `N8N_RUNNERS_SERVER_ENABLED`, which `FromEnv` defaults to 15 and sets to `true` in the launcher's environment (`tags/0.5.0-rc/internal/commands/launch.go` lines 70 to 72; `internal/env/env.go` lines 57, 166, 167 and 179; the same lines at 0.4.0-rc). 0.6.0-rc keeps the four names, that timeout and `allowed-env` and adds `N8N_RUNNERS_SERVER_ENABLED=true` (`tags/0.6.0-rc/internal/commands/launch.go` lines 54 to 58; `internal/env/env.go` lines 155 and 156). From 0.7.0-rc, `PrepareRunnerEnv` keeps the four names and `allowed-env` and adds a server-enabled flag, `N8N_RUNNERS_SERVER_ENABLED` at 0.7.0-rc and `N8N_RUNNERS_HEALTH_CHECK_SERVER_ENABLED` from 1.0.0, and the configured auto-shutdown timeout (`tags/0.7.0-rc/internal/env/env.go` and `tags/1.0.0/internal/env/env.go` lines 76 to 85). 1.1.0 to 1.1.5 also add the task timeout (`tags/1.1.5/internal/env/env.go` lines 86 to 101). No tag before 1.2.0 sets the task broker URI or the health-check port, removes an inherited required key before the first runner starts (apart from 0.2.0-alpha's grant token) or reads `env-overrides`, which no configuration before 1.2.0 defines (`tags/1.1.5/internal/config/config.go` lines 65 to 80). From 0.4.0-rc, the `env.Clear` call after each runner exits removes every `N8N_RUNNERS_GRANT_TOKEN` entry, including one inherited through `allowed-env`, so each later runner receives only its fetched grant token (`tags/0.4.0-rc/internal/commands/launch.go` line 150 and `tags/0.5.0-rc/internal/commands/launch.go` line 149; `internal/env/env.go` lines 97 to 107 at both). 1.2.0 matches the cited 1.3.1 behaviour (`tags/1.2.0/internal/config/config.go` lines 78 to 82) except that it sets no health-check port, which its required-key list also lacks (`tags/1.2.0/internal/env/env.go` lines 13 to 32 and 105 to 147). 1.3.0 behaves as 1.3.1 does, its allowlist match being a loop rather than `slices.Contains` (`tags/1.3.0/internal/env/env.go` lines 39 to 65 and 103 to 145). For the launch command, 0.1.0 to 0.1.1 insert no grant token and 0.2.0-alpha inserts one as above. 0.3.0-rc fetches one grant token from `N8N_RUNNERS_N8N_URI` with the auth token, appends `N8N_RUNNERS_GRANT_TOKEN`, sets the result as the command's environment and runs the runner once (`tags/0.3.0-rc/internal/commands/launch.go` lines 20 to 25 and 75 to 96). From 0.4.0-rc, each launch loop fetches a launcher grant token for the handshake and then the runner's, appends `N8N_RUNNERS_GRANT_TOKEN` to the prepared environment, sets that as the runner command's environment and clears the token after the runner exits. In each tag's `internal/commands/launch.go`, the cited lines are 85 to 126 and 150 at 0.4.0-rc, 85 to 126 and 149 at 0.5.0-rc, 71 to 118 and 141 at 0.6.0-rc, 38, 51 to 98 and 121 in the 0.7.0-rc and 1.0.0 file (read at 1.0.0), 38, 49 to 95 and 118 in the 1.1.0 to 1.2.0 file (read at 1.2.0), 47, 59 to 105 and 129 at 1.3.0, and 47, 59 to 105 and 130 in the 1.3.1 to 1.4.7 file (read at 1.4.7). The 1.3.1 to 1.4.7 file differs from the 1.5.0 and 1.5.1 file in shutdown-signal forwarding, dial-failure retries, the broker readiness poll interval and how the health monitor is started, not in grant-token handling (`tags/1.5.1/internal/commands/launch.go` lines 65 to 73, 97 to 102, 106 to 111, 136 to 150, 172 to 190 and 197 to 200). The grant-token fetch, insertion and clearing read the same in the 1.3.1 to 1.4.7 file and the 1.5.0 and 1.5.1 file (`tags/1.5.1/internal/commands/launch.go` lines 121, 155 to 164, 184 to 185 and 218 to 219; `tags/1.4.7/internal/commands/launch.go` lines 59, 84 to 93, 104 to 105 and 129 to 130). Against the cited 1.3.1 to 1.5.1 behaviour, what matters for the guide is that no tag before 1.2.0 has `env-overrides`, so the guide's advice to set runner allowlists in `env-overrides` needs launcher 1.2.0 or later; the guide's 1.5.1 statements stay accurate as scoped, and every tag keeps only named variables from the launcher's environment. This reading infers, from Go `encoding/json` and Rust serde defaults, which were not read, that a launcher before 1.2.0 ignores an `env-overrides` key. `n8n.md` section 5 revises `environment-launcher-tags` to record the 29 tags and that boundary, adding the `launcher-115` (1.1.5) and `launcher-120` (1.2.0) components and two Sources items with the keys `s9887312755d4` and `sb5f305c25a6a`, pinned at commits 64bcb118 and c0c861a1. All 29 tags, 0.1.0 to 1.5.1, fetched on 2026-10-26, were read, and the launcher-tags item stays open in TODO.md under 1.300 until the maintainer reads whether the reading of those 29 tags meets the ruling's bound. Untagged commits, releases after 2026-10-26, `config.go` beyond those two fields and whether the launcher in the 2.40.6 runners image reads `_FILE` variables stay outside the launcher-tags item, the last staying in TODO.md under 1.300. The header's claim count stays at 80; rebinding `body_sha256` and regenerating the header, summary and bundles go with this change. |
| 1.300 | `n8n.md`: environment, the qa-p233a source reading at n8n 2.40.6 (commit 024ee46b) of the `$vars` source, the RPC transport, item W and the direct `/proc` file-read primitives, partial. (M, M) | Done, #923. The qa-p233a checkout of n8n at commit 024ee46bdc6d57e5c47ef2c26bcac2b3a9efd827 (n8n 2.40.6), fetched on 2026-10-26, and the p219a pack's rendered task-runner environment-variable page, fetched on 2026-10-26 (`fetch.log` line 1), were read; nothing was built or run. For `$vars`, the node context's `startJob` passes its own `additionalData` through `startRunnerTask` to `TaskRequester.startTask` (`packages/core/src/execution-engine/node-execution-context/base-execute-context.ts` lines 424 to 447; `packages/cli/src/workflow-execute-additional-data.ts` lines 859 to 895). `TaskRequester.startTask` keeps that `additionalData` in the task data (`packages/cli/src/task-runners/task-managers/task-requester.ts` lines 90 to 129). On a runner's data request, the requester's response builder copies `additionalData.variables` into the response, which the stripper passes on unchanged (`task-requester.ts` lines 355 to 378; `data-request-response-builder.ts` lines 33 and 37 to 55; `data-request-response-stripper.ts` lines 32 to 41). `getBase` sets `variables` from `WorkflowHelpers.getVariables`, which returns a frozen object of the cached variables resolved for the workflow's project (`workflow-execute-additional-data.ts` lines 781 to 841; `packages/cli/src/workflow-helpers.ts` lines 438 to 451); other builders of `additionalData` were not traced. For the RPC transport, each bound helper calls `makeRpcCall` (`packages/@n8n/task-runner/src/js-task-runner/js-task-runner.ts` line 596), which sends a `runner:rpc` message through `send` over the WebSocket that the runner opened to the broker with its grant token (`packages/@n8n/task-runner/src/task-runner.ts` lines 122 to 130, 243 to 245 and 510 to 536). `makeRpcCall` settles when `handleRpcResponse` receives the matching `broker:rpcresponse`, resolving on `success` and rejecting otherwise (`task-runner.ts` lines 271 to 272 and 538 to 552). The broker's WebSocket server passes each parsed message from a registered runner to `TaskBroker.onRunnerMessage` (`packages/cli/src/task-runners/task-broker/task-broker-ws-server.ts` lines 159 to 199). `TaskBroker` forwards `runner:rpc` to the task's requester as `broker:rpc` and a `requester:rpcresponse` to the task's runner as `broker:rpcresponse`, which the WebSocket server sends (`task-broker.service.ts` lines 274 to 280, 327 to 353 and 459 to 485; `task-broker-ws-server.ts` lines 149 to 151). That requester is the in-process `LocalTaskRequester`, which registers a callback with the broker and is bound as `TaskRequester`, and the requester dispatches `broker:rpc` to `handleRpc` (`task-managers/local-task-requester.ts` lines 29 to 39; `task-runner-module.ts` lines 94 to 100; `task-requester.ts` lines 284 to 285). `handleRpc` ignores a call for an unknown task, answers `Method not allowed` for a name outside `AVAILABLE_RPC_METHODS` and `Could not find method` when the path is missing from the task's `executeFunctions`, and otherwise converts serialized buffers, awaits the call and answers `success` with its result or `error` with what it threw (`task-requester.ts` lines 396 to 470). For item W, the rendered page says that, unlike the main n8n image, file-based configuration for secrets cannot be used in the task runner image, so variables with a `_FILE` suffix will not be recognized, as the guide's reading of the export of 2026-10-24 records. Both runners' own readers recognize such a variable: Python `read_env` reads the file that the `_FILE` variable names when the variable itself is absent (`packages/@n8n/task-runner-python/src/env.py` lines 5 to 19). The JS runner's settings use the `@Config` and `@Env` decorators of `@n8n/config` (`packages/@n8n/task-runner/src/config/js-runner-config.ts` lines 1 to 13; `base-runner-config.ts` lines 1 to 35), whose reader does the same (`packages/@n8n/config/src/decorators.ts` lines 19 to 37). The runners image's shipped `n8n-task-runners.json` lists no `_FILE` name in either runner's `allowed-env` or `env-overrides` (`docker/images/runners/n8n-task-runners.json` lines 13 to 31 and 39 to 53), and in internal mode neither spawn's explicit `env` copies one (`task-runner-process-js.ts` lines 53 to 91; `task-runner-process-py.ts` lines 36 to 59). The reading finds no conflict for the runner processes as shipped: the launch configuration, not the runner code, keeps `_FILE` variables from both runners, and this reading infers, without a run, that a `_FILE` name added to `allowed-env` would reach the runner's reader. In internal mode n8n copies the module allowlists, the Python settings and `N8N_RUNNERS_INSECURE_MODE` from its own `process.env`, so this reading infers that `N8N_RUNNERS_INSECURE_MODE_FILE` on n8n sets n8n's `insecureMode`, which drops the two JS spawn flags, but not the JS runner's own, which keeps prototype hardening (`task-runner-process-js.ts` lines 36 to 38 and 79). The JS runner sets its mode from its own `insecureMode` and applies prototype hardening only in secure mode (`js-task-runner.ts` lines 152, 160 and 190 to 192). Whether the launcher in the 2.40.6 runners image reads `_FILE` variables for its own settings was not read; the p219a pack's launcher tags page gives the 1.4.0 tag's commit subject as "feat: Support `_FILE` suffix for environment variables" (launcher pull request 86), so that part of W stays in TODO.md. For the `/proc` item, under the default allowlists and settings, the JS context binds `require`, a console, `$getWorkflowStaticData`, a fixed native list (`Buffer`, timers, `btoa`, `atob`, text codecs and `FormData`), the data proxy, the RPC functions and `additionalProperties`, and no `fs` or `process` (`js-task-runner.ts` lines 258 to 278 and 639 to 656). `require` loads a module only if its exact name is allowlisted or the list is `*` (`require-resolver.ts` lines 113 to 137); the allowlists are empty by default in internal mode and are `crypto` and `moment` in the shipped runners image (`js-runner-config.ts` lines 5 to 9; `n8n-task-runners.json` lines 27 to 31), so `fs`, `node:fs` and `child_process` are refused in both modes. The Python runner removes `open` from user builtins through its default deny list and replaces `__import__` and the `importlib` entry points with `_GuardedImport` (`constants.py` line 121; `task_runner_config.py` lines 117 to 128; `task_executor.py` lines 533 to 545 and 629 to 661). `_GuardedImport` refuses every module while both allowlists are empty, as they are by default and in the shipped image (`_sandbox_callables.py` lines 248 to 268; `import_validation.py` lines 24 to 47; `n8n-task-runners.json` lines 50 to 53). This establishes no direct file-read primitive under those defaults; indirect paths were not traced, including what `crypto` and `moment` expose, host-realm objects in the JS context, the seven helpers' n8n-side code and the `sys`, `linecache` and `importlib` modules that the Python executor keeps in `sys.modules` (`task_executor.py` lines 593 to 627). Whether a Code task can actually read `/proc/<pid>/environ` needs a run and stays in TODO.md under 1.384. `n8n.md` section 5 gains the claims `environment-js-rpc-transport`, `runner-file-reconcile` and `environment-proc-primitive`, and revises `environment-js-rpc` and `runner-file-secrets`. Seven new Sources items add the `runtime` keys `sf286998f3097`, `s8b1fb5bb9cd5`, `sb821c7acc0f5`, `sd96fb165e769`, `se418055d6a62`, `se75d1331e759`, `sebd37e717fee`, `sc105c5461dfd`, `seba45f867a0b`, `s53ff48afd944`, `s5f75fef8e83d` and `sef6f579fbcdb`. The `$vars` and RPC-transport items close, the `/proc` item's direct primitives close and W closes except for the launcher's own `_FILE` reading; the helpers' code, the indirect `/proc` paths, G and the launcher tags stay open in TODO.md under 1.300. The header's claim count goes from 77 to 80; rebinding `body_sha256` and regenerating the header, summary and bundles go with this change. |

| 1.301 | `observability-components.md`: the generated `Send` methods, the ingester's stream interceptors, `ServerStream.Write`, the transport `write` and `toRPCErr` for the ingester's empty-batch send, from the 2026-10-25 p206a pack, partial. (M, M) | Done, #897. The p206a pack holds Loki v3.7.8's `pkg/logproto/logproto.pb.go`, `pkg/ingester/ingester.go`, `pkg/loki/modules.go` and `pkg/loki/loki.go`, six vendored grpc-go and six vendored dskit files, the recursive tree and the v3.7.8 tag ref, which resolves to commit 09e6ce2f, fetched on 2026-10-25 between 14:30:22Z and 14:30:27Z. It also holds four files fetched between 14:58:55Z and 14:58:56Z: grpc-go's `server_stream.go`, `flowcontrol.go` and `controlbuf.go` and dskit's `server/limits.go`. Nothing was built or run. The integrator's comparisons with the pack's `SHA256SUMS` (`sha256-check.txt`) found all files matching, 18 at 14:42:55Z and 22 at 14:58:56Z. As source reading, the generated `Send` methods of the `Query` and `QuerySample` server streams return `SendMsg`'s result unchanged (`logproto.pb.go` lines 6183 to 6185 and 6204 to 6206), and dskit's logging and instrumentation stream interceptors pass the stream and the handler's error through unchanged. So does the in-flight limiter stream interceptor (`limits.go` lines 140 to 150), which dskit adds only when code sets `GrpcMethodLimiter`, a field with no YAML key (`server.go` lines 170 and 483 to 488). Loki adds a recovery stream interceptor on every target (`loki.go` line 513), query-tags, HTTP-header and ingestion-policy ones on the `ingester`, `write` and `all` targets and a query-limits one when per-request limits are enabled (`modules.go` lines 2195 and 2202 to 2207), and passes the server configuration to `fakeauth.SetupAuthMiddleware` (`loki.go` line 496). The per-request-limit and target conditions for those `modules.go` appends are at `loki.go` lines 873 to 895 and 914 to 917. The code of those Loki interceptors and of `fakeauth.SetupAuthMiddleware`, and the OpenTracing interceptor dskit adds when a global tracer is registered, lie outside the pack, so whether any of them wraps `SendMsg` is not established. Those Loki interceptors are only the ones that `loki.go` and `modules.go` show, and whether other code appends stream interceptors to dskit's `GRPCStreamMiddleware` is not established either. The server options that code sets in dskit's `GRPCOptions`, a field with no YAML key, are appended to dskit's own (dskit's `server.go` lines 124 and 529), and grpc-go's `NewServer` applies global server options before them (grpc-go's `server.go` lines 80 to 82 and 706 to 708); either can add a stream interceptor, and whether Loki code or a dependency sets them is not established. `ServerStream.Write` only returns its transport's `write` result (`server_stream.go` lines 69 to 72), and the HTTP/2 server transport sets itself as each new stream's transport (`http2_server.go` lines 402 to 407). That transport's `write` (lines 1159 to 1188) and its first header write check the stream's state, not its context: for a stream marked done they return `ErrConnClosing` if the transport's `done` channel is closed, otherwise `ContextErr` of the context's error (lines 1010 to 1024), and `write` does the same after a failed write-quota wait. `Close` marks the transport closing before it closes that channel (lines 1299 and 1304), so such a write in between takes the `ContextErr` path. `ContextErr` gives a `Canceled` or `DeadlineExceeded` status for those two context errors and an `Internal` status for any other value, including none (`transport.go` lines 863 to 872). In this guide's reading, every path in `http2_server.go` that marks a stream done, through `finishStream` or `closeStream`, cancels its context first (lines 1341 and 1343, 1365 and 1370). `toRPCErr` (`rpc_util.go` lines 1133 to 1157) turns that connection error into an `Unavailable` status and returns status errors unchanged, so, in this guide's reading and unless an unread interceptor intervenes, such a send that reaches `Write` fails with a gRPC status error, never the exact value `context.Canceled`, and the ingester code returns it. `finishStream` and `closeStream` cancel the context created with the stream (`http2_server.go` lines 535 to 539). `streamContextErr` reads the stream's current context (`http2_server.go` line 1016), which grpc-go's `handleStream` replaces through `SetContext` with one built from the original and passed through any configured stats handler's `TagRPC` (grpc-go's `server.go` lines 1801 to 1853, `server_stream.go` lines 123 and 124), so which status code results is not established. Either way the send fails with a gRPC status error, as above. A send reaches `Write` only if `SendMsg`'s message preparation succeeds: `SendMsg` returns an error from `prepareMsg` as is before that (the p196a pack's `stream.go` lines 1760 to 1763). The Loki section's empty-batch passage now says so, and `loki-ingester-empty-send` is revised. The empty-batch reading assumes grpc-go's HTTP/2 server transport: grpc-go's `ServeHTTP` builds a different transport on Go's own HTTP/2 server (grpc-go's `server.go` lines 1114 to 1124), and that transport's code was not read. Five more add `s369533e16eb3`, `s334ac63c0892`, `s68c48fdded52`, `sbf9e7851df52` and `s326c544e4f05` for grpc-go's global server option hook, `NewServer`'s option order and its stream interceptor options, and for dskit's `GRPCOptions` field and its append before `grpc.NewServer`. | 1.291 | `pocketbase.md`: Appwrite 2.2.0 web installer database selection, Compose generator database handling, value-less `_APP_DB_ADAPTER` representation and utopia-php/system empty-value reading from the 2026-10-25 p205a pack, partial. (M, M) | Done, #896. The p205a pack holds 60 Appwrite 2.2.0 files under `src/Appwrite/Platform/Installer/`, `src/Appwrite/Docker/` and `app/views/install/`, and utopia-php/system's recursive tree and `src/System/System.php` at bd8f3561, which 2.2.0's `composer.lock` pins for 0.10.6, fetched on 2026-10-25 between 14:06:08Z and 14:06:30Z; nothing was run. The drafting worker did not compare the files with the pack's `SHA256SUMS`; the integrator's comparison with `SHA256SUMS` found all 62 matching. As source reading of it with the p183a pack, the web installer's first step offers PostgreSQL, MariaDB and MongoDB, each only when enabled, preselecting a locked database or else `_APP_DB_ADAPTER`'s default as the install task passes it, and its script posts the choice as `database` with a `postgresql` fallback. The install handler accepts only those three values, requires an unlocked choice to be supported and enabled, and sets `_APP_DB_ADAPTER` to the locked database or else the choice (`Http/Installer/Install.php` line 241), which the install task keeps, passes to the Compose generator and writes to `.env` unless it reuses an existing configuration. When the installer server finds an existing Compose or environment file and has no locked database, it locks one detected from database service or container names in the Compose text, then from the environment file's `_APP_DB_ADAPTER` or `_APP_DB_HOST`. The generator replaces an unknown `database` value with `postgresql`, removes the other database services and their volumes, and replaces dependencies on database services or `${_APP_DB_HOST:-postgresql}` with one on the selected service under `service_healthy`. It never refers to `environment` or `_APP_DB_ADAPTER`, so this guide reasons that the rendered file keeps the template's value-less entry; no rendered file was observed. `Service` joins a service's `environment` list with newlines for `Env`, which skips a line without `=`, so a value-less `_APP_DB_ADAPTER` entry yields no key and the install task's first detection step, an `isset` test, does not match it. At bd8f3561, `System::getEnv` returns `getenv($name) ?: $default`, so an empty `_APP_DB_ADAPTER` takes the same fallback as an unset one, `postgresql` for the pools and `mariadb` for the console endpoint. `pocketbase.md` section 3 now says so in two new paragraphs and a revised empty-value sentence, the source-work row and conclusion paragraph name the remaining gaps, and the claims `source-limits-appwrite-console` and `source-limits-appwrite-install-env` are revised. Twenty-seven new Sources items add the `appwrite` keys `sad8784f8e9a9`, `s06b2c6eab43d`, `s805902cec1bc`, `s6fb9b6903781`, `s6c1141485824`, `s7d56eff0a7da`, `s9abe0896e1d5`, `s98f550167154`, `sae5eb7c54f35`, `s5bf1ec01b06d`, `sc45b0bcd7e3d`, `s743783a2e7c4` and `s447a96e44d6b`. They also add `sc7eff8d8ae01`, `s7bba5f7d026a`, `s65c6e1b53177`, `s83e6c05eb3d7`, `s20f9a45c1ae4`, `s1b716f6425c4`, `s4de85dfdcb6c`, `s62081c1c2ce8`, `s61db43082b23`, `sa319cbeaf14d`, `s0397bb7a7d9a`, `sd2a7f2cd5865`, `s681a4ae9171a` and `s6295037b83da`. One more Sources item adds the new component `utopia-php-system` (utopia-php/system, basis 0.10.6) with the key `s432a4a7a96bf`. No claim was added, so the header's claim count is unchanged; the new component changes the generated header's versions sentence, so replacing the top block with `top_block`, rebinding `body_sha256` and regenerating the summary and bundles go with this change. The deployment's adapter, Compose's resolution of the value-less entry, an observed installed Compose file's environment entries and the remaining transaction-state layers, including the handler's `processDocument` step, whose parent class file neither pack holds, stay open in TODO.md under 1.291. |

As source reading, `Detect` returns no finding for a file on the global path allowlist, builds a keyword set by running a prefilter of the configuration's keywords over the lowercased fragment, and runs a rule that has keywords only when one of them, lowercased, is in that set (`detect/detect.go` lines 177 to 209). For each regex match, `detectRule` then skips a line carrying `gitleaks:allow` unless `IgnoreGitleaksAllow` is set, takes the secret from the capture groups, and drops the match on a rule or global allowlist regex, then on a rule or global stopword (`detect/detect.go` lines 289 to 346). Each allowlist regex tests the captured secret by default, or the whole regex match or its line when that rule's or the global allowlist's `RegexTarget` is `match` or `line` (lines 321 to 339). Only when the rule's `entropy` is nonzero (`if rule.Entropy != 0.0 {`, line 351) does it then drop the match when its Shannon entropy is not above that value, and, inside the same condition, for a rule ID starting with `generic` also when the secret has no digit from 1 to 9 (lines 349 to 368; `detect/utils.go` lines 43 to 60 and 180 to 189). `ContainsStopWord` matches each stopword as a case-insensitive substring (`config/allowlist.go` lines 61 to 69). `machine-auth.md`'s default-rule coverage item now says so, its `generic-api-key` sentence says an entropy above 3.5 rather than a minimum entropy of 3.5, and the claim `verify-key-coverage` is revised. Three Sources items add the `gitleaks` keys `s7e8d49968e7b`, `s93e2b8afe3e2`, `sb475192e7bfd` and `s3db5f83b981f`. As this guide's inference, the emulated result (only `generic-api-key`, after a keyword assignment) still follows from the code, but the guide does not record whether its counts applied the strict comparison and the digit check. The pack holds no `config/gitleaks.toml`, so the default rule's keywords, entropy and secret group were not reread. On that platform `load_default_certs()` loads the `CA` and `ROOT` stores through `_load_windows_store_certs()` before calling `set_default_verify_paths()`, which it calls on every platform, and `ssl.py` never calls `enum_crls`. `_load_windows_store_certs()` loads each `x509_asn` certificate whose trust is `True` or includes the requested purpose's OID, and warns on an `SSLError` for one certificate or a `PermissionError` while enumerating the store. In `_ssl.c`, `enum_certificates` and `enum_crls` are compiled only under `_MSC_VER`, `ssl_collect_certificates()` collects the named store, read-only, from whichever of seven system store locations it can open, and the trust value is `True` when neither an enhanced key usage property nor an extension is found. A case-insensitive search of `ssl.py` and `_ssl.c` for apple, macos, mac os, osx, darwin, keychain and framework matched only a socket comment and a docstring in `ssl.py`, so neither file has macOS-specific store code. `python.md` section 4 now says so in a new paragraph, its assessment no longer lists platform-specific stores as unestablished, and six new Sources items add the `cpython-ssl` keys `s7d193a7cf5de`, `s71c79ecb9452`, `s27320e4a48e0`, `sade5cc44fad7`, `seb67fe33e276` and `sf95db151011c`, which the revised claim `client-ca` maps. | 1.291 | `pocketbase.md`: Appwrite 2.2.0 install task command-line selection and document GET transaction-state branches from the 2026-10-24 p183a pack, partial. (M, M) | Done, #876. The p183a pack holds Appwrite 2.2.0 and utopia-php/database `54364c68` files and the Appwrite 2.2.0 recursive tree, fetched on 2026-10-24 between 21:05:59Z and 21:06:04Z; nothing was run. As source reading of it, the install task starts the web installer when its `interactive` parameter is `Y`, the console is interactive and no argument starts with `--`, and otherwise takes its command-line path. For an existing installation it first detects the database from the first Compose service setting `_APP_DB_ADAPTER`, then `.env`, then a `mariadb`, `mongodb` or `postgresql` service name, then a service's `_APP_DB_HOST`, before the web-installer branch, which receives the result; in the command-line path it skips the interactive database question when it finds an existing Compose file. `Install.php` line 562 defaults `$database` to `postgresql` when the prepared input lacks `_APP_DB_ADAPTER` and line 642 passes it to the Compose generator, confirming the p122a report. Unless its `no-start` parameter is set, the task runs `docker compose` with `--env-file` and, unless it reuses an existing configuration, each nonempty prepared value assigned in the command's environment. The upgrade task hands its detected or assumed engine to the install task, whose own detection can still replace the assumed MariaDB. With a transaction ID, the document GET's transaction state reads the transaction and its `transactionLogs` entries inside `skip` and replays them only for a `pending` transaction; it returns staged creates from that state and overlays staged updates and upserts on a database-layer fetch, returning a staged upsert when that fetch is empty. This guide reasons that staged creates bypass database-layer retrieval and that, when a committed fetch returns empty, the upsert branch returns the staged document without applying another database-layer read check to it; the handler's `processDocument` step is not traced, and the cited lines compare no transaction creator with the requester. `pocketbase.md` section 3 now says so, the source-work row and conclusion paragraph name the remaining gaps, and the claims `appwrite-read-path` and `source-limits-appwrite-install-env` are revised. Twelve new Sources items add the `appwrite` keys `s1c354b6df527`, `s21476585e7d9`, `s4daf9abb72b4`, `s8a44ff629c40`, `s08213afcf157`, `s2a7f2bc4e834`, `sde11935a99de`, `s48bedca5fd4a`, `sb04bd8725358`, `s72ba13c41e7e`, `s3afe73884566` and `s5625c02a35f4`. No claim was added, so the header's claim count is unchanged; rebinding `body_sha256` and regenerating the summary and bundles go with this change. The deployment's adapter, the web installer's selection, the Compose generator's output, Compose's resolution of the value-less `_APP_DB_ADAPTER` entry and the remaining transaction-state layers stay open in TODO.md under 1.291; the environment documentation page was not fetched (resolver block), so its recorded conflict was not rechecked. |

As documentation reading of the p180a pack, whose Markdown exports were fetched on 2026-10-24 at 19:27:02Z, the set-up page links the hardening page and the task-runner environment-variable page and says task runners are the only isolation layer between user-provided code and n8n. Its quotations were also checked against its rendered HTML, fetched at 19:26:50Z; the other two pages' rendered HTML was not fetched. The set-up page says that without task runners, or with internal mode, anyone who can edit a workflow could potentially read the database, encryption key, stored credentials and environment variables. Its launcher section gives the configuration file's container path as `/etc/task-runners.json` and its mount target as `/etc/n8n-task-runners.json`, the second matching the guide's image reading (DONE 1.333). The hardening page recommends external-mode sidecars, the distroless image, the `nobody` user (65532), a read-only root filesystem and an AppArmor rule denying reads of per-process `environ` and `mounts` files, which it says can expose environment variables to code in the container. It also says Code node executions are secure and that external runners provide a fully isolated environment for the Code node's JavaScript, without defining that isolation. The environment-variable page documents `N8N_RUNNERS_INSECURE_MODE`, `N8N_RUNNERS_ALLOW_PROTOTYPE_MUTATION` and `N8N_RUNNERS_ALLOW_TRANSITIVE_IMPORTS`, each defaulting to `false`, says `_FILE` variables are not recognized in the task runner image, and says the launcher provides `N8N_RUNNERS_GRANT_TOKEN`. None of the three pages names `N8N_BLOCK_ENV_ACCESS_IN_NODE`, `$env` or `process.env`, or says which variables from the n8n or runners container reach a runner beyond the launcher's `allowed-env` and `env-overrides`, which does not establish that no others do. As source reading at n8n 2.40.6 (024ee46b), with nothing run, the internal spawns set no `uid` or `gid` option, and each start creates a new runner ID and grant token and points the runner at the broker on `127.0.0.1`. By the same reading, the internal Python spawn also copies `N8N_RUNNERS_ALLOW_TRANSITIVE_IMPORTS` and `N8N_RUNNERS_BUILTINS_DENY`, and Python tasks run in `forkserver` subprocesses. Also by that reading, the task runner module starts internal runners only in `internal` mode and skips the Python runner when its requirements check fails, and `insecureMode` drops the internal JS spawn's two hardening flags and the JS runner's prototype hardening. The tie from `insecureMode` to `N8N_RUNNERS_INSECURE_MODE` rests on the documentation and existing `js-runner-config.ts` citation; it was not traced in this pack. `n8n.md` section 5 gains the claims `runner-insecure-mode`, `runner-file-secrets`, `python-transitive-imports`, `runners-hardening` and `runners-isolation-statements`, two `docs` Sources items (`sbc0e98df861a`, `s2f18871e2911`) and one `runtime` item (`scaa2ea88798e`). No existing claim text, status or Verify mapping changed; rebinding `body_sha256` and regenerating the header, summary and bundles go with this change. This guide's assessment: the ECR tag was then Docker Hub's image, but the annotations are producer metadata and tags move, so the build from that recipe and any later pull stay NOT ESTABLISHED. As attestation data reading, GitHub's attestations API served one SLSA provenance v1 statement for that digest, naming `.github/workflows/release.yaml` at `refs/tags/v2.45.1` and commit 11d2eeb5. The pack's `git ls-remote` printed 11d2eeb5 for that tag and no `^{}` line, so the tag names that commit directly. At the 11:38Z fetch the statement's Sigstore bundle could not be verified: tuf-repo-cdn.sigstore.dev, rekor.sigstore.dev and fulcio.sigstore.dev resolved to `::` under a LAN resolver block on the network that built the pack, and `gh attestation verify` exited 1. After a maintainer DNS allow for sigstore.dev, the p166a pack's verification reading at 2026-10-24T12:51Z, with gh 2.101.0, records `gh attestation verify --owner dexidp --format json` exiting 0 with one verification result: issuer `https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com`, signer workflow `artifacts.yaml` at `refs/tags/v2.45.1`, source dexidp/dex at 11d2eeb5 and run 22625708938. By a hash comparison made for this change, its DSSE payload, signature and certificate are those of the p164a statement. The same command without `--format json` also exited 0 but printed nothing, so the JSON run is the record of what was checked. That verification checks the bundle against the Sigstore public-good trust root and the `--owner dexidp` constraint, not the workflow file's content, so in this guide's assessment that digest came from that release run, while what a later pull of the tag returns is not established. The Redis and Dex prose, the Redis listener table row, the Verify scope note, the Sources preface and the `redis-image-name` and `dex-image` claims now say so, with no new Sources URL or component. The 12:51Z verification came after #862 merged and is recorded here in place by #865, with the Dex prose, the Verify scope note, the Sources preface and the `dex-image` claim updated to match. Rebinding `body_sha256` and regenerating the summary and bundles go with this change. The Redis build and later pulls, the Redis environment copy and per-handler RBAC coverage stay open in TODO.md under 2.28. |
| 1.306 | `self-signed.md`: debian-trust and rhel-trust, currently supported releases as of 2026-10-24, Debian forky, Ubuntu questing and Fedora 45's release status on that date, partial. (M, M) | Done, #861. As documentation reading of the 2026-10-24 source pack, Debian's releases page says the current stable distribution is version 13, trixie, and lists bookworm as the current oldstable release, with End of Life 2026-07-11, now passed, and End of LTS 2028-06-30. Its bullseye row gives End of LTS as 2026-08-31, also passed, while that row's Status cell still says bullseye is under LTS support, on a page whose footer gives Last Modified as 2026-06-17 and Last Built as 2026-09-23. It lists forky, version 14, as "testing" with "no release date has been set", and buster and stretch as archived releases under third-party paid extended LTS support. Ubuntu's meta-release feed marks trusty, xenial, bionic, focal, jammy, noble and resolute "Supported: 1" and questing "Supported: 0", and Ubuntu's release-cycle page says "Ubuntu 26.10 was released in October 2026", a release the feed does not list. That page gives coverage after standard security maintenance only through Ubuntu Pro: focal's Expanded Security Maintenance runs to May 2030 and bionic's to May 2028, while xenial and trusty are covered after May 2026 and May 2024 only "With legacy add-on", to May 2031 and May 2029. Red Hat's errata page says "Red Hat Enterprise Linux versions 8, 9, and 10 deliver a ten-year life cycle" and lists python 3.12 (RHEL 10) among its urgent bug fix inclusions, but its life-cycle dates table is absent from the fetched HTML. It says customers on legacy extended support offerings such as RHEL 6 and 7 "can continue using them through their committed end dates", which the pack does not give. The pack's fetches of Fedora's Bodhi release list, Fedora's releases page, the dl.fedoraproject.org `releases/` and `development/` listings and the archives.fedoraproject.org releases listing all returned bot-challenge pages titled "Making sure you're not a bot!", which `fetch.log` records with HTTP 200. Its CentOS Stream page has empty download sections and no release dates. As data reading, the orchestrator's rsync listing of Fedora's master mirror (dl-rdu01) at 2026-10-24T10:50:30Z shows `releases/` holding directories 7 to 44 and `test`, and `development/` holding 45 and `rawhide`; the listing is not recursive, so it does not show what `test` holds. The mirrors.kernel.org `fedora/releases/` index lists the same directories, 7 to 44 and `test`. Both listings give directory modification times, not release dates (in the rsync listing, 42 is dated 2026/07/02 and 43 is dated 2025/10/25, while the kernel.org index dates 42 as 03-Jul-2026), and neither carries a support or end-of-life field. This guide's assessment: Fedora 45 was not a released Fedora when the listing was taken, since the master mirror placed it under `development/` beside rawhide and not under `releases/`. The listings do not establish which Fedora releases are supported, since `releases/` still holds 7 to 44 and the orchestrator's README notes that the master tree keeps old releases. The mirror.stream.centos.org index lists `9-stream` and `10-stream` and no `8-stream`, under a banner saying "This directory tree contains new CentOS Stream releases, starting from release '9-stream'". The vault.centos.org index lists `8-stream` (modified 2024-06-03) and neither `9-stream` nor `10-stream`, under a banner calling the vault "a snapshot of the older trees that have been removed from the main CentOS servers" and saying "we do not put security updates into the trees on this server". This guide's assessment: CentOS Stream 8's tree has moved to the vault while 9 and 10 remain on the active mirror, but neither index states which CentOS Stream releases are supported, so the pack does not establish that a mirror's directory set is a support statement. This guide also assesses that Debian trixie and bookworm (in LTS by its dates), the Ubuntu releases the feed marks supported, and RHEL 8, 9 and 10 are currently supported, and that bullseye's Debian LTS has ended by its dated column. By the same assessment, extended LTS, which Debian's page calls third-party paid support for buster and stretch and dates to 2031-06-30 in bullseye's End of ELTS column, is not Debian support, so it brings none of buster, stretch and bullseye into this row. Forky, which has no release date, and questing, which the feed marks unsupported, are not currently supported releases, so neither needs a citation under this row (this guide's assessment); the forky manpage URL again redirected to `testing`, and the questing manpage URL returned 404. Fedora's and CentOS Stream's supported releases and the status of RHEL 6 and 7 are not established by the pack. Of the currently supported releases the guide does not cite, the pack sources no trust-store command for RHEL 10 (its documentation index returned 403) or for Ubuntu trusty, xenial and bionic, so the guide keeps its qualifiers. Whether coverage given only through Ubuntu Pro counts as current support was an open maintainer question when this row was written, covering trusty, xenial, bionic and the cited focal, whose standard security maintenance Ubuntu's release-cycle page gives as ending in May 2025. The maintainer ruled on 2026-10-24 that it does not, standard support only (DECISIONS.md), so for those four releases that ruling supersedes this row's assessment above: trusty, xenial and bionic need no citation, and focal is a cited release past standard support, like bullseye, whose citation the guide keeps. Ubuntu 26.10 is newer than every cited release, so it is routine refresh work under the maintainer ruling, not this row. The maintainer's refined ruling at 2026-10-24T13:29:48Z (DECISIONS.md) supersedes #861's records-only assessment for bullseye: #864 adds dated guide readings noting bullseye as past Debian LTS and focal as past standard security maintenance, keeping both citations because they still document those releases' commands. This guide's assessment: the guide's Fedora 45 qualifier, "release status not established", and its CentOS Stream 8 citation stay, since the listings record one moment, Fedora 45 may move to `releases/` at any time, and the CentOS Stream 8 citation still documents that release's commands. No guide text, claim, component, Sources item or `body_sha256` changed in #861, whose assessment was that nothing in the pack made what the guide said wrong. The refined ruling is implemented by #864's guide notes and lifecycle Sources items. The two lifecycle Sources items are deliberately unkeyed because no claim states support status. In #861, TODO.md kept 1.306 open, rewritten in place, for the RHEL 10 and Ubuntu trusty, xenial and bionic trust-store sources, Fedora and CentOS Stream support status, RHEL 6 and 7, and a recheck of Fedora 45. In #864, Ubuntu trusty, xenial and bionic were removed from that remaining-work list under the ruling. |

Each image has one layer, whose `bin/nc` hard-links to `bin/[`, a BusyBox v1.36.1 binary. Each binary carries `^np:s:ubvw:+lki:o:z`, `?2:vv:ll` and `nuvlkz`, and none carries the small nc's `lp:w:i:f:e:`. Matched against source reading of 1.36.1 (`nc_bloaty.c` lines 779 and 793 to 801, `nc.c` lines 58 to 59 and 136 to 137), that is the `NC_110_COMPAT` nc built with `NC_SERVER` and `NC_EXTRA`. Source reading of `libbb/getopt32.c` lines 67 to 69 gives `w:+` a nonnegative integer parameter. That this nc's option parser accepts `-z` and such a `-w` is this guide's inference, not a reading: matching a string literal does not show which function references it, and no disassembly was done. The pack's source clone checks out only the networking, include, libbb and scripts directories, in which `np:s:ubvw:+` occurs only at `nc_bloaty.c` line 794, so whether the string occurs elsewhere in BusyBox was not checked. The usage text each binary packs, re-derived here by bzip2 decompression and byte-identical to the pack's `usage.bin` (`usage.txt` is that text with NUL separators as newlines), gives the 1.10-compatible nc usage with `-w SEC` and `-z` on all nine platforms, but BusyBox generates it from the build configuration, so it corroborates the build options and does not show the parser. The index's amd64 entry (manifest `sha256:b7f3d86d6e84fc17718c48bcde1450807faa2d56704205c697b4bd5df7b9e29f`) carries the annotations `org.opencontainers.image.revision` c1375496 and `org.opencontainers.image.version` 1.36.1-glibc, matching the guide's docker-library/busybox commit and glibc variant. In #868 the guide describes the binary string as matching `nc_bloaty.c`'s option string, its only match in the directories read, keeping the parser inference and its limits, and the `probe-image` claim now says the string matches `nc_bloaty.c`'s. As documentation reading of the Heroku Dev Center pages checked in October 2026, no page says whether the Generations page's Cedar mark for "Heroku ACM wildcard domains" covers Cedar Private Spaces or only Cedar apps outside them. The Feature Parity table has no legend or footnote on what a column covers, and its wildcard row links the custom-domains page's wildcard section, which names no generation, runtime or ACM. The same column marks the "Common Runtime" row, and the Internal routing, VPC peering, VPN support and Private Spaces DNS Service Discovery rows, where Fir has To Be Added; the Private Spaces page lists those four features under "Additional Features for Cedar Private Spaces". None of the three fetched articles calls its feature Private-Spaces-only, though the DNS Service Discovery article says "Unlike dynos in the Common Runtime, dynos in Private Spaces can freely exchange TCP and UDP traffic on a private network." This guide's assessment: the Generations page's "Here are some of the key benefits of using Fir compared to the Cedar version of Private Spaces:" and, under "Cedar-Only Features" after the table, "Some features from Cedar-generation Private Spaces have been or will be replaced by improved Fir-only features" point to Private Spaces, but its "Common Runtime" row bars a Private-Spaces-only column. Line 31 and the Generations Sources item now say so in place of "remains unresolved", splitting each of their assessment sentences in two, and a new Sources item cites the Private Spaces page, whose URL enters the `heroku` component as `sa8e24c10ccd5` and the `managed-tls` sources. | 1.341 | `gitops-controllers.md`: grpc-go v1.81.1 peel record and global server options, the remaining part of the bug21955 workaround interceptor Sources label row (#800 r2 QA, both families; #820 r1 QA). (L, S) | Done, #854. Recorded the reference-pack peel, dated 2026-10-24: the p155a pack's `git ls-remote` of grpc-go `refs/tags/v1.81.1*`, run by the orchestrator at 2026-10-24T06:05:21Z, printed one line, `caf0772c2bcb8bc15d43eb53448e921f34f0b7e8 refs/tags/v1.81.1`, and no `^{}` line, so v1.81.1 is a lightweight tag naming caf0772c directly, the commit the #800 citations assume. As source reading of grpc-go v1.81.1 without a run, `NewServer` applies `globalServerOptions` before the caller's options (`server.go` lines 693 to 698), and only the `internal.AddGlobalServerOptions` hook that `server.go`'s `init` sets appends to that list (lines 79 to 81; declared at line 199). In the same reading, a global `ChainUnaryInterceptor` option would be appended ahead of the caller's interceptors (line 487) and a global `UnaryInterceptor` prepended to the chain (lines 1216 to 1217), so either would run ahead of Argo CD's bug21955 workaround. As source reading of Go's internal-package rule in a local go1.26.5 toolchain tree (`cmd/go/internal/load/pkg.go` lines 1547 to 1561, not compared with upstream), module code may import `google.golang.org/grpc/internal` only from import paths under `google.golang.org/grpc`, which include nested modules such as `gcp/observability`, not only the grpc module's own packages. A search of the pack's module cache for `AddGlobalServerOptions` found the pack's five listed files, all in grpc v1.81.1 (`server.go`, `internal/internal.go`, `stats/opentelemetry/csm/observability.go` and two `_test.go` files), so within the grpc module the hook's one non-test caller is the exported `csm.EnableObservability` (lines 41 to 57), and no `experimental` package calls it. The hook's comment (`internal/internal.go` lines 85 to 86) says the 1.0 release of `gcp/observability`, a separate module the cache does not hold, uses it. In the pack's `go list -deps ./...` listing for Argo CD c9c369ef (go1.26.5, linux/amd64, default tags, cgo on, test-only imports not listed, nothing built or run), all 85 of its 2380 packages under `google.golang.org/grpc` belong to grpc v1.81.1 and none is `csm` or under `gcp/observability`. A search of the Argo CD tree found no `AddGlobalServerOptions`, `EnableObservability` or `go:linkname`. This guide's assessment: no package in that graph calls grpc-go's global server options hook, so the label's "first in the unary chain" holds there; other tags, cgo-off builds, platforms and test builds, `unsafe` or reflective writes, and runtime behaviour were not checked. The label now names that scope and its limits, and a new Sources item, which states the same limits and the hook's comment, cites `server.go#L79-L84` and `stats/opentelemetry/csm/observability.go#L41-L57` at caf0772c as `grpc-go` keys `s0fdc41207dd8` and `s5a39fca6ceeb`, both mapped to `argo-auth-overrides`, whose text, status and components are unchanged. No claim text, status or components changed, rebinding `body_sha256` for the Sources edits goes with this change, and nothing remains under 1.341. After #854 r2 QA (both families), the new Sources item was split into one item per URL, the two keeping between them every qualifier and limit it stated, with the same URLs and keys, both still mapped to `argo-auth-overrides`. After #857 r1 QA (both families), the label and the second of those items say the graph was listed with cgo on, and rebinding `body_sha256` goes with that edit. After #859 r1 QA, #865 split the label at its parenthesis into two sentences, the second stating that its "first in the unary chain" is this guide's source assessment, with every qualifier, URL, key and claim mapping unchanged. |

| 3.45 | A CHANGELOG format gate, proposed after a CHANGELOG heading without a blank line after it slipped through on 2026-10-23: check a blank line after each `## YYYY-MM-DD` heading, strictly descending headings, and one heading per date. (L, S) | Done, #837. Added `tools/check_changelog_format.py`, which reads CHANGELOG.md only and fails a `## YYYY-MM-DD` heading not followed by an empty line, a dated heading newer than the one above it, a repeated date, a heading that is not a calendar date, and a line that opens like a dated heading but is not exactly one. A missing, non-UTF-8 or undated changelog fails closed. On LF input, it reads dated headings with the pattern that `latest_change()` in `tools/version_basis.py` uses; `latest_change()` reads with `read_text`, which translates CR line endings, while the gate splits on LF alone, so a CRLF-saved changelog that `latest_change()` reads fails the gate (#837 r1 QA). It is a separate gate rather than an extension of `tools/check_changelog_prs.py`, which reads the git history and fails on a shallow clone. It does not model fenced blocks, so a dated heading line inside one counts as a heading and over-flags; CHANGELOG.md has no fenced block at 48cf9718. Added `tools/test_changelog_format.py`, which runs the shipped gate on throwaway trees with failing fixtures and passing twins for each of the three rules, wired both into `tools/run_all_checks.sh` self-test first, and named the gate in CONTRIBUTING.md's "Shipping a change" section. Normalized the six headings the row names, 2026-09-19 to 2026-09-14 (CHANGELOG.md lines 714, 731, 764, 810, 824 and 860 at 48cf9718; lines 692, 709, 742, 788, 802 and 838 at 09e91b5d), by inserting the missing blank line after each, with no bullet text changed. A read-only scan of CHANGELOG.md at 48cf9718 with the gate's heading patterns found those six and no other violation of the three rules among its 31 dated headings. |

Cited the supervised separate `ollama serve` process, which the app restarts and starts with `OLLAMA_HOST=0.0.0.0` when its Expose setting is on, the spawn copying the app's own environment, so that setting or an inherited `OLLAMA_HOST` can expose it. | 1.334 | `observability-components.md`: Loki inverted ingester interval, entries at its start. (L, S) | Done, #806. Established, by source reading of Loki v3.7.8's push validation without a Loki run, that an ingester can hold an entry timestamped exactly at the future start of the inverted ingester interval that the querier sends for a negative store lookback other than `-1` when now minus it is after the selection end. The distributor validates each pushed entry against a context built from `time.Now()` (`pkg/distributor/distributor.go` lines 591 to 592): `ValidateEntry`'s only timestamp checks reject a timestamp strictly newer than now plus the tenant's `creation_grace_period`, so one exactly at that bound passes, and one older than now minus `reject_old_samples_max_age` (`pkg/distributor/validator.go` lines 101 to 113); the defaults are 10m for `creation_grace_period` (`pkg/validation/limits.go` lines 353 to 354), true for `reject_old_samples` (line 327) and 7d for the maximum age (lines 351 to 352), and a case-insensitive search of `pkg` for `reject_new`, `RejectNew` and `reject-new` found none (the future check above is unconditional). After validation, with `increment_duplicate_timestamp` on (default false, `pkg/validation/limits.go` line 328), the distributor sets each different-line duplicate after the first to the previous stored entry's timestamp plus one nanosecond (`pkg/distributor/distributor.go` lines 686 and 749 to 758), so a push of n such entries at the bound can store the last one n-1 nanoseconds past it. The ingester's own push chain (`Ingester.Push`, `instance.Push`, `stream.Push`) compares entry timestamps with stream state and the schema configuration that stream creation reads (`pkg/ingester/instance.go` lines 316 and 423 to 440), never the clock: duplicates of the last line, rate limits, a too-far-behind drop below the stream's highest timestamp minus half `max_chunk_age` (default 2h, `pkg/ingester/ingester.go` line 162) with unordered writes (default true, `pkg/validation/limits.go` line 362), and ordered appends rejecting entries older than the head block's newest (`pkg/chunkenc/memchunk.go` lines 187 to 190) or, with the head block empty and cut blocks present, the previous block's maximum (lines 872 to 874), so, in source, that push chain imposes no clock-relative upper bound on a stored entry's timestamp, which storage keeps as Unix nanoseconds (`pkg/chunkenc/memchunk.go` line 869). In the guide's assessment, when the negative lookback's magnitude is at most the tenant's creation grace period, the interval's start (now minus the lookback) lies at or before the latest timestamp the distributor accepts at the query's moment, so an ingester can hold an entry dated exactly at that start; the comparison assumes one reference clock and an unchanged grace period, since an entry keeping its submitted timestamp must pass a validation whose now plus the grace period is at or past that start. For a larger magnitude, a direct ingester gRPC push is one route the source establishes; the Kafka partition-ingester consumer pushes each consumed record into the ingester's `Ingester.Push`; the consumer stores that ingester as its pusher (`pkg/ingester/ingester.go` line 395, `pkg/ingester/kafka_consumer.go` lines 53 to 71 and 150 to 159), and the duplicate-timestamp shifts are not ruled out. Added `loki-push-future-bound` (392 characters), `loki-ingester-push-validation` (399) and `loki-ingester-inverted-hold` (399) with 23 new source keys, dropped the untraced-holding clause from `loki-ingester-inverted-time-filter` (388 to 342) and revised `loki-querier-ingester-inverted-result` (374 to 400) to keep only the unobserved runtime result open. What the ingester querier and ingesters return at runtime, and the Kafka consumer and partition-ingester ingestion paths, remain open in TODO.md under 1.301. |

| 1.301 | `observability-components.md`: Loki inverted ingester interval return path at v3.7.8, index lookup, iterators, empty-batch send and in-tree `Ingester.Wrapper` reads, partial. (M, M) | Done, #802. Traced, as source reading of Loki v3.7.8 without a Loki run, what the ingester does with the inverted interval when no wrapper is configured. Its index lookup uses the interval's start only to pick the index of the schema period containing it (for a future start, the latest period starting at or before it), every stream is added to each index the ingester builds, and the inverted and bit-prefix lookups match label matchers and shard without reading time, so the interval's order causes no lookup error. No stream, chunk or head-block iterator swaps or clamps the interval, and the ordered head block's log iterator drops every entry for it. The chunk's time-range filters accept an entry timestamped exactly at the start before testing the end, so, as this guide's assessment, they pass only entries dated exactly at that future start. Read the vendored go-datastructures v1.1.7 range tree and its entries pool: the unordered head block queries it from the interval's start to one nanosecond before its end, both inclusive, so the query returns no entry for the inverted interval and backward log queries, which do not time-filter the head block, get nothing from it. When the ingester's iterator yields nothing without an error and the request context is not done, it sends one batch with no streams or series and returns no error unless that send returns an error other than the exact value `context.Canceled`; a context already done before the send skips it with no error, and that code never aborts a send in progress. What the send returns if the context ends during it is not traced. The ingester querier's per-ingester iterator yields nothing and ends without an error at the stream's end, with any other receive error propagating, and the querier merges those iterators with any store query it makes. A `git grep -w Wrapper` of the `pkg`, `cmd`, `integration`, `tools` and `clients` trees at v3.7.8 found only the field's declaration (`yaml:"-"`), its `Wrap` interface and `initIngester`'s nil check and `Wrap` call, besides a code comment and two Bootstrap source maps. Added `loki-ingester-inverted-lookup` (377 characters), `loki-ingester-inverted-iterators` (353), `loki-ingester-inverted-time-filter` (388), `loki-ingester-inverted-unordered` (345), `loki-ingester-empty-send` (395), `loki-querier-inverted-merge` (338) and `loki-ingester-wrapper-search` (377) with 37 new source keys, and narrowed `loki-querier-ingester-inverted-result` (400 to 374 characters) to name entries dated exactly at the interval's start as the untraced remainder. What the ingester querier and ingesters return at runtime, wrappers set outside the searched trees and the rest of 1.301 remain open in TODO.md under 1.301; whether an ingester can hold an entry dated exactly at that future start is new row 1.334. |

| 3.41 | `tools/version_basis.py`: fail, never baselined, a component source URL that pins a ref while that component's basis is `unknown`, so a pinned source lands in a component whose basis is that pin. A pin is a commit SHA or tag in a forge `/blob/`, `/tree/`, `/raw/`, `/blame/`, `/commit/` or `/releases/tag/` path (GitLab-style `/-/` paths included), the ref segment of a `raw.githubusercontent.com` URL, a cgit `h=` or `id=` query, or a GitHub wiki revision; `main`, `master`, `HEAD`, `trunk` and `develop` refs and release-line documentation paths such as `/docs/2.4/` are not pins. At commit 0310e00d it fails eight URLs in seven guides: `caddy.md`, `elasticsearch.md` (two), `headless-browser-services.md`, `memcached.md`, `minio.md`, `rabbitmq.md` and `vector-databases.md`; move each, with its claim references, in the same change. Regression cases in `tools/test_version_basis.py` must fail with the check removed. Follows row 3.39. (M, S) | Done, #630. The offline lexical guard rejects only `unknown` bases for recognized pins; it does not compare a known basis with the URL ref. It treats pin-shaped branches as pins. It cannot identify digit-free tags in generic paths or SHAs under seven characters as SHAs (digit-bearing values may still match the tag shape), and it does not cover other cgit views, Gitea `/src/tag/`, `/releases/download/`, Bitbucket or googlesource paths. |

| 1.141 | Create-once key writers (`model-servers.md`'s llama.cpp, SGLang, vLLM and text-generation-webui blocks, and the LobeChat and Stable Diffusion WebUI writers in `chat-uis.md` and `image-gen-uis.md`): a default ACL on the target directory is inherited by new files and defeats `umask 077`, so a key file can be group-readable while the guide says `0600`. Refuse a directory carrying a default ACL (`getfacl`) and assert the file's mode after the write, verifying setfacl/getfacl behaviour first (from the #355 review, maintainer ruling 2026-09-25). (L, S) | Done, #498. All seven writers (text-generation-webui has two) refuse, before the write, a target directory for which `getfacl -d -c -p` prints a default ACL, and fail closed when `getfacl` fails or is not installed; after the write they fail unless `ls -ln` shows `-rw-------` and tell the reader to delete the file. Recorded first with acl 2.3.2: a file created under `umask 077` in a directory given `setfacl -d -m u::rw,g::r,o::r` was mode `0644`, `chmod 700` left an inherited default ACL in place, and `setfacl -k` removed it; each writer as it stood wrote its file mode `0644` under a default ACL on `~/.config`. |

| 2.13 | New guide: transactional email authentication posture | Done, #132. Added `transactional-email-posture.md`, the corpus's first SMTP/DMARC content, standards-based rather than product-based (numbered sections). Covers SPF (RFC 7208: `-all`/`~all`/`+all` terminators, the ten-lookup permerror budget that a long `include` chain silently blows, the multiple-record permerror), DKIM (RFC 6376 selector record and empty-`p` revocation, RFC 8301 `rsa-sha256` with a 1024-bit floor and a 2048-bit recommendation, selector rotation), DMARC (a pass needs one aligned SPF or DKIM result against the visible `From:`; `p`/`sp`/`np` fallback; DNS tree walk), DMARC reporting (aggregate `rua` first per RFC 9990; failure `ruf` deferred per RFC 9991 because it can leak a live reset token), SMTP submission and relay (587 STARTTLS is stripping-vulnerable, 465 implicit TLS is not, TLS with hostname validation before AUTH, and open-relay rejection per RFC 2505 while ordinary local delivery stays legitimate), and transport and display (MTA-STS with the delivery-to-not-from direction caveat per RFC 8461, TLS-RPT reporting-only, BIMI as non-auth display metadata). Material standards correction, verified at source: DMARC is now RFC 9989 (May 2026), obsoleting RFC 7489, splitting reporting into 9990/9991, removing `pct`, and adding `np`; the guide cites the current RFCs and names `pct` only as legacy, catching the stale-prior trap the deep-facts seed was built to surface. Verify reasoned (no DNS/SMTP/runtime): `dig` and `openssl s_client` inspection, no TLS-skip; row 2.31 tracks the live demo. Added exposure-index rows 25/465/587. Deep-facts codex seed front-loaded the record depth, gemini sharpened the STARTTLS-stripping framing, combined by Fable, every record verified at the RFC source, tri-family QA. (M, M) |
